\title{A Textbook about the Paper: "Group Sequence Policy Optimization"}

# Chapter 1: Introduction to Reinforcement Learning in Large Language Models

Reinforcement learning (RL) has rapidly emerged as a pivotal paradigm for scaling the capabilities of large language models (LLMs). As these models grow in size and complexity, static supervised fine-tuning alone proves insufficient for unlocking their full potential in tackling sophisticated, open-ended problems. Through large-scale RL, language models develop the ability to engage in deeper and longer reasoning processes, enabling them to solve competition-level mathematics, write complex code, and navigate intricate logical chains that were previously beyond their reach. This shift represents a fundamental evolution in how we train AI systems, moving from merely predicting the next token to actively optimizing for high-quality outcomes verified by external evaluators. However, as researchers attempt to push these boundaries by investing greater computational resources into RL training, they encounter a formidable barrier: the requirement for extremely stable and robust training dynamics. Without stability, the massive investments in compute yield diminishing returns or, worse, catastrophic failures.

## The Role of RL in Scaling LLM Capabilities

The primary driver behind integrating reinforcement learning into the training pipeline of modern large language models is the necessity to handle tasks that require extended reasoning horizons. Traditional autoregressive training objectives optimize for local coherence—predicting the most likely next token based on immediate history—but this myopic view often fails when a task requires planning several steps ahead or correcting early mistakes. RL addresses this by treating text generation as a sequential decision-making process where the model acts as an agent interacting with an environment defined by a query and a verifier.

In this framework, an autoregressive language model parameterized by $\theta$ is defined as a policy $\pi_\theta$. When presented with a query $x$, the model generates a response $y$, which can be viewed as a sequence of tokens $y_1, y_2, \dots, y_{|y|}$. The likelihood of generating this specific response under the policy is calculated as the product of conditional probabilities at each step: $\pi_\theta (y | x)=\prod_{t=1}^{|y|} \pi_\theta (y_t | x, y_{<t} )$, where $|y|$ denotes the total number of tokens in the response. Crucially, once a complete response $y$ is generated for a query $x$, it is evaluated by a verifier function $r$. This verifier assigns a scalar score $r(x, y) \in [ 0, 1 ]$ representing the quality or correctness of the entire sequence. By maximizing the expected reward over many generations, RL guides the policy toward producing responses that are not just linguistically fluent but logically sound and factually accurate regarding complex domains like advanced mathematics and software engineering. This capability allows models to undertake "deeper" reasoning processes that are essential for solving problems where intermediate steps must be coherent before reaching a final conclusion.

## The Crisis of Training Stability: Model Collapse and Irreversibility

Despite the promise of RL for scaling intelligence, practical implementation at massive scales has revealed severe fragility in current training methodologies. The foremost prerequisite for successfully scaling RL with increased computational investment is maintaining stable training dynamics; however, state-of-the-art algorithms often fail to meet this requirement when applied to gigantic language models. The consequence of this instability is frequently "catastrophic model collapse," a phenomenon where the model's performance degrades rapidly and irreversibly during training. Once this collapse occurs, it manifests as an irreversible loss of capability that cannot be remedied simply by reverting to previous checkpoints or meticulously tuning hyperparameters such as clipping ranges or extending generation lengths. Even changing the set of queries used during training has been observed to be ineffective once collapse sets in.

This instability creates a critical bottleneck in pushing the capability boundaries of language models. If every increase in model size or dataset complexity carries a non-negligible risk of triggering an irreversible collapse that wastes weeks of computation and energy reserves, then true scaling becomes impossible. The root cause of these failures often lies hidden within the algorithmic design choices made during optimization. Specifically, when training very large models or Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures on long-response tasks using standard approaches like Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), researchers have observed that small variances in gradient estimates can compound over time. This accumulation leads to erratic policy updates that eventually destabilize the entire system. Understanding why these collapses happen is not merely an academic exercise but a necessary step toward designing algorithms that can reliably harness massive compute clusters without fear of self-destruction during training runs.

## Limitations of Existing Algorithms: The Flaws in GRPO

To understand why current methods struggle with stability at scale, one must examine the mechanics of Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), which was designed to eliminate the need for separate value networks required by earlier methods like Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO). GRPO attempts to simplify RL by estimating advantages relatively within a group of responses generated for the same query rather than relying on an auxiliary value model. In GRPO, given a query $x$, $G$ responses $\{y_i\}_{i=1}^G$ are sampled from an old policy $\pi_{\theta_\text{old}}$. The advantage $\widehat{A}_{i}$ assigned to each response is computed based on its reward relative to other responses in the group:
$$ \widehat{A}_{i} = \frac{r(x, y_i) - \mathrm{mean} \left( \{ r(x, y_i) \}_{i=1}^G \right) }{ \mathrm{std} \left( \{ r(x, y_i) \}_{i=1}^G \right) } $$
All tokens within a specific response $y_i$ share this same advantage value $\widehat{A}_{i}$. The optimization objective then sums over all tokens across all groups:
$$ \mathcal{J}_\text{GRPO}(\theta) = \mathbb{E}_{ x \sim \mathcal{D},\,\{y_i\}_{i=1}^G }
\left[ \frac{1}{G} \sum_{i=1}^{G} \frac{1}{|y_i|} \sum_{t=1}^{!|y_i|} 
\min \left( w_{i,t}(\theta) \widehat{A}_{i},  \mathrm{clip}(w_{i,t}(\theta), 1 - {\varepsilon}, 1 + {\varepsilon})\widehat{A}_{i}\right)\right] $$
Here lies the fundamental flaw identified in recent research: GRPO applies importance sampling weights at the **token level**. The importance ratio for each token $t$ is defined as:
$$ w_{i,t}(\theta)=\frac{ \pi_{\theta} (y_{i,t} | x,y_{i,<t}) }{\pi_{\theta_\text{old}}(y_{i,t}|x,y_{i,<t}) } $$
The principle of importance sampling dictates that re-weighting samples from a behavior distribution $\pi_\text{beh}$ to approximate expectations under a target distribution $\pi_\text{tar}$ requires averaging over many samples ($N \gg 1$). Mathematically:
$$ \mathbb{E}_{z ~sim~ pi_\text {tar }}[f(z)] = E_z sim pi _ {beh}[ frac { pi _ {tar }( z )}{ pi _ {beh }( z ) } f(z)] $$
However, GRPO calculates this weight based on a single sample $y_{i,t}$ drawn from each next-token distribution $\pi_{\theta_\text {old}}( . | x , y _ { i , < t })$. Because there is only one sample per token position being considered at any moment during backpropagation relative to that specific distribution slice, the weight fails to perform its intended role of correcting distributional mismatches effectively. Instead of reducing bias introduced by off-policy data collection (where responses are generated by $\pi_{\theta_\text {old}}$ but updated using gradients from $\pi_\theta$), these token-level weights introduce high-variance noise into the training gradients.

This noise becomes particularly destructive because it accumulates over long sequences and interacts poorly with the clipping mechanism inherent in PPO-style algorithms. As training progresses on long-response tasks common in reasoning benchmarks like AIME or CodeForces, these accumulated errors amplify until they trigger model collapse. Furthermore, this issue is exacerbated in Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models due to "expert-activation volatility." In MoE architectures, different subsets of experts are activated depending on input routing decisions driven by gating networks trained via softmax distributions subject to floating-point precision limits and stochasticity between inference engines and training engines. Consequently, even if two policies are nearly identical parameters-wise ($	 ext { theta } approx   ext { theta } _ old ), they might activate completely different expert paths for certain inputs causing drastic fluctuations in calculated token-level probabilities—and thus wildly unstable importance ratios—which renders GRPO ineffective without complex workarounds like "Routing Replay" strategies that artificially constrain expert selection consistency across iterations—a solution that adds significant overhead while limiting model capacity utilization rates unnecessarily since real-world deployment doesn't enforce such rigid routing constraints anyway anyway! Thus existing approaches suffer fundamentally ill-posed objectives unsuitable stable large-scale applications requiring robustness against natural variations encountered during prolonged iterative # Chapter 2: Foundations of Policy Optimization Algorithms

Autoregressive language models function fundamentally as probabilistic policies, mapping inputs to sequences of outputs. When we speak of training these models via reinforcement learning (RL), we are essentially refining a policy $\pi_\theta$ parameterized by weights $\theta$. In this framework, an input query is denoted as $x$, drawn from a dataset $\mathcal{D}$. The model generates a response $y$, which consists of a sequence of tokens $y_1, y_2, \dots, y_{|y|}$, where $|y|$ represents the total number of tokens in the response. The probability of generating this specific sequence under the current policy is defined as the product of conditional probabilities for each token given the preceding context. Mathematically, this sequence likelihood is expressed as $\pi_\theta (y | x)=\prod_{t=1}^{|y|} \pi_\theta (y_t | x, y_{<t} )$. Here, $y_{<t}$ denotes all tokens in the response prior to index $t$. This autoregressive structure implies that the probability of the entire sequence is decomposable into a chain of local token predictions. Each complete query-response pair $(x, y)$ is subsequently evaluated by a verifier or reward model $r$, yielding a scalar reward $r(x, y) \in [ 0, 1 ]$ that quantifies the quality or correctness of the response. Understanding this foundational notation is critical before examining how different algorithms optimize these policies.

## Autoregressive Policies and Likelihood Notation

The core mechanism driving modern large language models is their ability to predict the next token in a sequence based on all previous tokens. This process transforms the model into a stochastic policy $\pi_\theta$ used in reinforcement learning contexts. When analyzing the behavior of such a policy, we must distinguish between the probability assigned to individual tokens and the joint probability assigned to an entire generated sequence. The notation $\pi_\theta (y_t | x, y_{<t})$ refers to the probability mass assigned by the model to a specific token $y_t$ at step $t$, conditioned on the user query $x$ and all previously generated tokens up to step $t-1$. Because language generation is sequential, the likelihood of producing a full response $y$ is not simply an independent sum of token probabilities but a multiplicative accumulation. As defined earlier, this global sequence likelihood is calculated as $\pi_\theta (y | x)=\prod_{t=1}^{|y|} \pi_\theta (y_t | x, y_{<t} )$.

This distinction between token-level and sequence-level probabilities becomes increasingly significant as response lengths grow. In short responses, the product involves few terms, keeping values relatively manageable. However, for long-chain reasoning tasks common in advanced RL applications—such as solving competition-level mathematics or writing complex code—the product spans hundreds or thousands of terms. Consequently, small variations in individual token probabilities can compound exponentially when calculating the total sequence likelihood. This compounding effect creates a highly non-linear landscape for optimization algorithms. Any method attempting to adjust policy parameters must account for whether it treats every token independently or views the generation as a holistic event governed by its aggregate likelihood. The choice between these perspectives dictates how importance sampling weights are constructed and how gradients flow back through time during training.

## Review of PPO: Importance Sampling, Clipping, and the Value Model Bottleneck

Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) stands as one of the most influential algorithms in deep reinforcement learning and serves as a primary reference point for subsequent developments like GRPO and GSPO. The central philosophy behind PPO is to perform policy updates that stay within a "proximal" region relative to an older version of itself, denoted as $\pi_{\theta_\text{old}}$. This constraint prevents destructive updates where new policies diverge too rapidly from old ones, which could lead to catastrophic forgetting or unstable training dynamics. PPO achieves this through an objective function that incorporates an importance sampling ratio and a clipping mechanism. The standard PPO objective seeks to maximize expected returns while penalizing deviations from the old policy beyond a certain threshold $\varepsilon$. Formally, this objective is written as:

$$
\mathcal{J}_\text{PPO}(\theta) = \mathbb{E}_{ x \sim \mathcal{D},\, y \sim \pi_{\theta_\text{old}}( \cdot | x) }
\left[ \frac{1}{|y|} \sum_{t=1}^{|y|} 
\min \left( w_{t}(\theta) \widehat{A}_{t},  \mathrm{clip} \left( w_{t}(\theta), 1 - {\varepsilon}, 1 + {\varepsilon}\right) \widehat{A}_{t} \right)
\right]
$$

In this equation, several components interact dynamically. The term $w_{t}(\theta)$ represents the importance ratio at token level $t$, defined as $w_{t}(\theta) = \frac{ \pi_{\theta} (y_{t} | x, y_{<t}) }{ \pi_{\theta_\text{old}} (y_{t} | x,y_{<t}) }$. This ratio measures how much more likely (or unlikely) the current policy considers generating token $y_t$ compared to what was predicted by the old policy during data collection. The variable $\widehat{A}_{t}$ denotes the advantage estimate for that specific token position, indicating how much better or worse that action was compared to average behavior at that state. Crucially, PPO requires estimating these advantages using an auxiliary value model—a separate neural network trained alongside the policy to approximate expected future rewards from any given state.

The reliance on this value model constitutes a significant bottleneck in scaling PPO for large language models. Firstly, constructing a value model with comparable capacity to the massive policy network doubles memory consumption and computational costs during training batches. Secondly—and perhaps more critically—the efficacy of PPO hinges entirely on the accuracy of these value estimates. Estimating values reliably for long-horizon tasks involving complex reasoning chains is notoriously difficult; errors in value estimation propagate through advantage calculations ($\widehat{A}_{t}$), introducing noise that can destabilize training even if other hyperparameters are tuned perfectly. As models scale in size and task complexity increases toward multi-step reasoning problems requiring extensive rollouts, acquiring precise value estimates becomes increasingly challenging, limiting PPO's practical utility for training giant language models efficiently. Despite its theoretical elegance regarding trust regions via clipping mechanisms dependent on importance ratios like $w_t(\theta)$ applied per-token rather than globally across sequences remains # Chapter 2: Foundations of Policy Optimization Algorithms

Before diving into Group Sequence Policy Optimization (GSPO), it is essential to review necessary theoretical background concerning existing reinforcement learning algorithms used for training large language models (LLMs). This chapter explores Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) and its reliance on auxiliary value models before transitioning toward Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). Students will learn how GRPO attempts circumvent dependence upon explicit value functions by leveraging group-relative advantages computed across multiple responses generated per query yet retains problematic token-level importance weighting schemes ultimately revealing fundamental flaws therein paving way understanding why newer approaches like GSPO were developed addressing these shortcomings directly through novel architectural innovations grounded firmly mathematical principles governing importance sampling theory applied appropriately within context autoregressive generation tasks # Chapter 3: Diagnosing Instability – Why Token-Level Weighting Fails

The pursuit of scaling reinforcement learning (RL) for large language models has revealed a critical bottleneck: the inherent instability of current state-of-the-art algorithms when applied to massive architectures and long-horizon tasks. While algorithms like Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) were designed to eliminate the need for auxiliary value models, empirical evidence has shown that they frequently suffer from catastrophic and irreversible model collapse, particularly when training gigantic models or Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures. This chapter dissects the root causes of this failure. We argue that the instability is not merely a hyperparameter tuning issue or a side effect of architectural complexity, but rather stems from a fundamental theoretical misapplication of importance sampling within the GRPO framework. By examining the mathematical disconnect between token-level weighting and sequence-level rewards, we will understand how high-variance noise accumulates over long sequences and interacts destructively with clipping mechanisms to destabilize training dynamics.

## Theoretical Requirements for Valid Importance Sampling vs. Token-Level Application

To understand why GRPO fails, one must first revisit the foundational principles of importance sampling. In statistical estimation, importance sampling allows us to approximate the expectation of a function $f$ under a target distribution $\pi_\text{tar}$ by drawing samples from a different behavior distribution $\pi_\text{beh}$. The mathematical identity governing this relationship is expressed as:

$$ \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \pi_\text{tar}} \left[ f(z) \right] = \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \pi_\text{beh}} \left[ \frac{ \pi_\text{tar} (z) }{ \pi_\text{beh}(z)} \, f(z) \right] $$

Here, the term $\frac{ \pi_\text{tar} (z) }{ \pi_\text{beh}(z)}$ acts as an importance weight that corrects for the discrepancy between the two distributions. Crucially, for this estimator to be unbiased and effective, it relies on averaging over a sufficiently large number of independent samples ($N \gg 1$) drawn from $\pi_\text{beh}$. As the number of samples increases, the law of large numbers ensures that the weighted sum converges to the true expectation under $\pi_\text{tar}$. The validity of this method depends entirely on the ability to aggregate many samples to smooth out stochastic variations in individual weights.

GRPO attempts to apply this logic at the granularity of individual tokens within a generated sequence. In this formulation, for every token $y_{i,t}$ in a response $y_i$, an importance weight is calculated as:

$$ w_{i,t}(\theta)=\frac{ \pi_{\theta} (y_{i,t} | x, y_{i,<t}) }{ \pi_{\theta_\text{old}} (y_{i,t} | x,y_{i,<t})} $$

This weight represents the ratio of probabilities assigned by the current policy $\pi_\theta$ versus the old policy $\pi_{\theta_\text{old}}$ for generating exactly that single token given its history. However, this application violates the core requirement of importance sampling because it treats each token position as if it were an independent sample sufficient for correction. In reality, at any specific time step $t$, we only have **one** sample ($y_{i,t}$) drawn from the conditional distribution $\pi_{\theta_\text{old}}( \cdot | x, y_{i,<t})$. There is no ensemble of tokens at position $t$ to average over; we are attempting to estimate a distributional shift based on a single data point. Consequently, the weight $w_{i,t}$ does not function as a reliable correction factor but rather acts as a source of high-variance noise. Because there is no aggregation over multiple tokens at a specific position to cancel out stochastic fluctuations, these weights fail to perform their intended distribution-correction role, rendering the objective function ill-posed for single-sample token-level updates.

## Analysis of High-Variance Noise Accumulation in Long-Sequences Generation

The failure of token-level importance weights becomes increasingly severe as sequence length grows. In language generation tasks involving complex reasoning or coding, responses can span hundreds or thousands of tokens. When GRPO calculates gradients using token-level weights $w_{i,t}$ multiplied by advantages $\widehat{A}_{i,t}$, it implicitly assumes that these weights accurately reflect how likely a trajectory would be under the new policy relative to the old one. However, since each weight is derived from a single sample without proper averaging, they contain significant stochastic error.

As training progresses through long sequences, these errors do not remain isolated; they compound multiplicatively and additively depending on how gradients are aggregated across tokens. Consider a response consisting of $|y|$ tokens. If each token's probability ratio carries even slight variance due to sampling noise or minor shifts in local context distributions, multiplying these ratios together—or summing their logarithmic contributions—amplifies the total variance exponentially with respect to sequence length. The paper notes that this high-variance training noise progressively accumulates with increased response length. In short responses, this accumulation might be negligible enough for training to appear stable temporarily. However, in long-response tasks typical of advanced reasoning benchmarks like competition-level mathematics or programming code generation, the accumulated noise becomes dominant over any genuine learning signal derived from rewards.

This accumulation creates a feedback loop where unstable gradients cause erratic updates to model parameters $\theta$. These erratic updates further distort future probability distributions $\pi_\theta$, which in turn generates even more volatile importance weights in subsequent steps because the divergence between $\pi_\theta$ and $\pi_{\theta_\text{old}}$ increases unpredictably at every step along with longer contexts eventually leads to what researchers observe as catastrophic model collapse. Once this collapse initiates—a state where performance degrades irreversibly—it cannot be recovered simply by reverting checkpoints or tuning hyperparameters like clipping ranges because the underlying statistical assumption required for stable gradient estimation has been fundamentally violated throughout the entire trajectory generation process. Therefore what begins as manageable noise transforms into destructive interference preventing any coherent optimization path forward during extended generations necessary for sophisticated problem-solving capabilities demanded today's largest language models require sustained stability across vast temporal horizons which simple per-token corrections cannot guarantee due their inability handle cumulative uncertainty inherent sequential decision-making processes governed probabilistic policies alone insufficient basis constructing robust estimators without aggregating multiple realizations per timestep—which autoregressive decoding inherently precludes since only one token emitted sequentially at once time instant exists within any given realization trajectory sampled behavior policy thus making traditional PPO-style approaches unsuitable unless modified appropriately either via value functions approximating expected returns ahead OR shifting focus away entirely toward holistic measures encompassing whole trajectories rather fragmented components thereof failing capture global coherence needed successful scaling endeavors involving lengthy outputs characteristic modern generative AI applications ranging logical deduction creative writing technical documentation production etcetera all sharing common trait requiring accurate assessment entire should correspond accordingly hence motivating alternative designs prioritizing sequence-wide metrics over atomistic ones prone amplification artifacts arising solely mechanical composition rules # Chapter 4: The GSPO Algorithm – Core Principles and Formulation

The evolution of Reinforcement Learning (RL) for Large Language Models (LLMs) has reached a critical juncture where the stability of the training algorithm dictates the ceiling of model capability. As models grow in size and complexity, particularly with the adoption of Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures, traditional algorithms that rely on token-level approximations begin to fail. This chapter delves into the foundational architecture of Group Sequence Policy Optimization (GSPO), an algorithm designed to rectify the fundamental instabilities inherent in previous methods like Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). The central thesis of GSPO is a rigorous adherence to the statistical principle that the unit of optimization must align perfectly with the unit of reward. By shifting the locus of control from individual tokens to entire sequences, GSPO establishes a theoretically sound framework that enables stable, efficient, and scalable training for state-of-the-art language models.

## The Principle of Matching Units: Sequence Reward Requires Sequence Optimization

To understand why GSPO was necessary, one must first appreciate the structural flaw in its predecessors regarding the application of importance sampling. In standard RL settings for language models, a verifier assigns a scalar reward $r(x, y)$ to an entire response $y$ generated for a query $x$. This reward is inherently holistic; it evaluates the coherence, correctness, and quality of the complete sequence as a single entity. However, algorithms like GRPO attempted to optimize this global signal by breaking it down into local components. They assigned this single sequence-level advantage $\widehat{A}_i$ to every individual token $y_{i,t}$ within that sequence and then applied token-level importance weights $w_{i,t}(\theta)$ to correct for off-policy sampling errors.

This approach creates a fundamental mismatch between the unit of reward and the unit of optimization. The principle of importance sampling dictates that to estimate an expectation under a target distribution $\pi_\theta$ using samples from a behavior distribution $\pi_{\theta_\text{old}}$, one must re-weight samples based on their joint probability ratio $\frac{\pi_\theta(z)}{\pi_{\theta_\text{old}}(z)}$. Crucially, this correction relies on averaging over many samples from the behavior distribution to effectively cancel out distributional mismatches. In GRPO, however, the importance weight is calculated at each token position $t$ based on a single sample $y_{i,t}$ drawn from the conditional distribution $\pi_{\theta_\text{old}}(\cdot | x, y_{i,<t})$. Because this weight is derived from a single realization rather than an aggregate expectation, it fails to perform its intended role as a valid distribution-correcting factor. Instead, it acts as a source of high-variance noise.

As training progresses and response lengths increase, this noise accumulates exponentially along the sequence. When combined with the clipping mechanism—which truncates extreme weights—the result is not merely reduced variance but distorted gradients that can lead to catastrophic model collapse. Once such collapse occurs in GRPO-trained models, it is often irreversible; reverting checkpoints or tuning hyperparameters yields no recovery because the underlying gradient estimator has become fundamentally broken. GSPO addresses this by recognizing that since the reward is granted to the entire sequence, the off-policy correction must also be applied at the sequence level. By treating the response $y$ as an atomic unit for both weighting and clipping, GSPO ensures that the optimization objective respects the granularity of the feedback signal provided by the verifier. This alignment eliminates the accumulation of token-level noise and provides a stable foundation for scaling RL to massive models and long-context tasks.

## Defining the Sequence-Level Importance Ratio $s_i(\theta)$ via Geometric Mean Normalization

With established motivation for moving away from token-level weights, we turn to the mathematical formulation of GSPO's core component: the sequence-level importance ratio $s_i(\theta)$. In GRPO, the importance ratio for a token was defined simply as $w_{i,t}(\theta) = \frac{\pi_\theta(y_{i,t} | x, y_{i,<t})}{\pi_{\theta_\text{old}}(y_{i,t} | x, y_{i,<t})}$. If we were naively extended this logic to sequences without modification, we might consider using raw likelihood ratios $\frac{\pi_\theta(y_i | x)}{\pi_{\theta_\text{old}}(y_i | x)}$. However, such raw ratios present significant numerical challenges when dealing with autoregressive generation over hundreds or thousands of tokens. The product of probabilities across a long chain tends toward zero extremely rapidly due to floating-point underflow or explosive growth depending on implementation specifics. More critically for optimization stability, small changes in likelihoods at just a few positions can cause dramatic fluctuations in these raw products as sequence length varies.

To resolve these issues while maintaining theoretical validity regarding importance sampling theory (as referenced in prior literature), GSPO defines $s_i(\theta)$ using a length-normalized geometric mean approach. The formula is expressed as:
$$ s_i(\theta) = \left( \frac{\pi_\theta(y_i | x)}{\pi_{\theta_\text{old}}(y_i | x)} \right)^{\frac{1}{|y_i|}} $$
Expanding this definition reveals its relationship to token-level probabilities:
$$ s_i(\theta) = \exp \left( \frac{1}{|y_i|} \sum_{t=1}^{|y_i|} \log \frac{\pi_\theta(y_{i,t} | x, y_{i,<t})}{\pi_{\theta_\text{old}}(y_{i,t} | x,y_{i,<t})} \right) $$
This formulation essentially calculates the exponential of the average log-probability ratio across all tokens in the sequence. By raising likelihood ratios to their reciprocal length ($1/|y_i|$), GSPO transforms what would be an unstable multiplicative accumulation into a stable additive average in log-space before exponentiating back out. This geometric mean normalization serves two vital purposes aligned with intuition first described in Section 3a). First, it controls $s_i(\theta)$ within a unified numerical range regardless of whether a response consists of 10 tokens or 1000 tokens. Without this normalization, longer sequences would naturally produce vastly different magnitudes for their importance ratios compared to shorter ones solely due to length differences rather than actual policy divergence rates per step. Secondly—and perhaps most importantly—it reduces variance dramatically compared to summing independent random variables across long chains; averaging dampens outliers whereas multiplying them amplifies them exponentially over time steps tending towards infinity if not corrected appropriately beforehand through division inside exponentiation domain operations themselves! Thus providing robustness against stochastic fluctuations during early stages exploration phases before convergence achieved later iterations onwards consistently thereafter throughout entire
